=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a grid with H horizontal rows and W vertical columns.
Let (i, j) denote the cell at the i-th row (1\leq i\leq H) from the top and j-th column (1\leq j\leq W) from the left.
Initially, there is a slime with strength S _ {i,j} in cell (i,j), and Takahashi is the slime in the cell (P,Q).
Find the maximum possible strength of Takahashi after performing the following action any number of times (possibly zero):

- Among the slimes adjacent to him, choose one whose strength is strictly less than \dfrac{1}{X} times his strength and absorb it.
  As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and Takahashi's strength increases by the strength of the absorbed slime.

When performing the above action, the gap left by the disappeared slime is immediately filled by Takahashi, and the slimes that were adjacent to the disappeared one (if any) become newly adjacent to Takahashi (refer to the explanation in sample 1).

Input

The input is given in the following format from Standard Input:
H W X 
P Q
S _ {1,1} S _ {1,2} \ldots S _ {1,W}
S _ {2,1} S _ {2,2} \ldots S _ {2,W}
\vdots
S _ {H,1} S _ {H,2} \ldots S _ {H,W}

Output

Print the maximum possible strength of Takahashi after performing the action.

Constraints


- 1\leq H,W\leq500
- 1\leq P\leq H
- 1\leq Q\leq W
- 1\leq X\leq10^9
- 1\leq S _ {i,j}\leq10^{12}
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 3 2
2 2
14 6 9
4 9 20
17 15 7

Sample Output 1

28

Initially, the strength of the slime in each cell is as follows:

For example, Takahashi can act as follows:


- Absorb the slime in cell (2,1). His strength becomes 9+4=13, and the slimes in cells (1,1) and (3,1) become newly adjacent to him.
- Absorb the slime in cell (1,2). His strength becomes 13+6=19, and the slime in cell (1,3) becomes newly adjacent to him.
- Absorb the slime in cell (1,3). His strength becomes 19+9=28.

After these actions, his strength is 28.
No matter how he acts, it is impossible to get a strength greater than 28, so print 28.
Note that Takahashi can only absorb slimes whose strength is strictly less than half of his strength. For example, in the figure on the right above, he cannot absorb the slime in cell (1,1).

Sample Input 2

3 4 1
1 1
5 10 1 1
10 1 1 1
1 1 1 1

Sample Output 2

5

He cannot absorb any slimes.

Sample Input 3

8 10 2
1 5
388 130 971 202 487 924 247 286 237 316
117 166 918 106 336 928 493 391 235 398
124 280 425 955 212 988 227 222 307 226
336 302 478 246 950 368 291 236 170 101
370 200 204 141 287 410 388 314 205 460
291 104 348 337 404 399 416 263 415 339
105 420 302 334 231 481 466 366 401 452
119 432 292 403 371 417 351 231 482 184

Sample Output 3

1343

- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.

- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

```
import sys, heapq

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    H = next(it); W = next(it); X = next(it)
    P = next(it); Q = next(it)
    S = [[next(it) for _ in range(W)] for _ in range(H)]
    start_r = P-1; start_c = Q-1
    cur = S[start_r][start_c]
    added = [[False]*W for _ in range(H)]
    added[start_r][start_c] = True
    heap = []
    dirs = [(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)]
    for dr,dc in dirs:
        nr = start_r+dr; nc = start_c+dc
        if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W and not added[nr][nc]:
            added[nr][nc] = True
            heapq.heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nr, nc))
    while heap:
        val, r, c = heap[0]
        if val * X >= cur:
            break
        heapq.heappop(heap)
        cur += val
        for dr,dc in dirs:
            nr = r+dr; nc = c+dc
            if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W and not added[nr][nc]:
                added[nr][nc] = True
                heapq.heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nr, nc))
    print(cur)
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

If using idx, need get S value for neighbor: S[nr][nc]. We can compute nr = idx//W, nc = idx%W. On push, we have r,c from loop. On pop, val, idx = heappop(heap); r = idx // W; c = idx % W. Then for neighbors, compute nidx = nr*W+nc; if not added[nidx]: added[nidx]=1; heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nidx)). This tuple has two elements, less memory. Let's implement with bytearray and idx.

Pseudo final:
```
import sys, heapq

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W, X = map(int, input().split())
    P, Q = map(int, input().split())
    S = [list(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(H)]

    sr = P - 1
    sc = Q - 1
    cur = S[sr][sc]
    added = bytearray(H * W)
    added[sr * W + sc] = 1

    heap = []
    # initial neighbors
    for dr, dc in ((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)):
        nr = sr + dr; nc = sc + dc
        if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W:
            nid = nr * W + nc
            if not added[nid]:
                added[nid] = 1
                heapq.heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nid))

    while heap:
        val, nid = heap[0]
        if val * X >= cur:
            break
        heapq.heappop(heap)
        cur += val
        r = nid // W
        c = nid % W
        for dr, dc in ((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)):
            nr = r + dr; nc = c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W:
                nid2 = nr * W + nc
                if not added[nid2]:
                    added[nid2] = 1
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nid2))
    print(cur)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
Need note: We use `heap[0]` to peek. If heap empty, loop ends. Good.

Potential issue: If `val * X >= cur`, break. But what if there are multiple heap entries with same val? If min not absorbable, all others >= val, so none absorbable. Good.

Potential issue: We mark added when pushing. Suppose a cell is pushed but before it is popped, it becomes "internal"? Can a boundary cell become internal without being eaten? No, territory only grows by eating cells; an uneaten cell cannot become internal because it is not part of territory. It remains boundary if adjacent. So okay.

Potential issue: Suppose a cell is pushed as boundary, but later another cell is eaten and the first cell is no longer adjacent? Is that possible? If a cell is adjacent to territory through a particular eaten cell. Territory grows, so the eaten cell remains territory. Thus adjacency remains. If the first cell was pushed because adjacent to a cell that was in heap? Wait we only push neighbors when a cell is eaten. So the pushed cell is adjacent to an eaten cell (territory). That eaten cell remains territory forever. So the pushed cell remains adjacent. Good.

Potential issue: Starting cell's neighbors are pushed; they are adjacent to start territory. Good.

Before final, let's revisit the proof of greedy one more time, because if there is a hidden counterexample, the whole solution fails. Let's attempt to formalize with "frontier" and "monotonicity".

Define state as set T of absorbed cells, current strength C = sum_{v in T} S_v. Boundary B(T) = {u not in T : adjacent to some v in T}. Action: choose u in B(T) with S_u * X < C, new T' = T ∪ {u}, C' = C + S_u. Since S_u >0, C' > C. Also T ⊂ T'. Boundary B(T') = (B(T) \ {u}) ∪ (neighbors of u not in T'). Actually B(T') includes B(T) minus u plus new neighbors. Since T' superset T, any v in B(T) \ {u} remains adjacent to T' (because adjacent to some w in T ⊂ T'), so B(T) \ {u} ⊆ B(T'). Thus no boundary cell is lost except the absorbed one. This is key.

Now, suppose at state T, there is an absorbable boundary cell m with minimal S_m. Let an optimal continuation from T absorb a set A eventually (maybe not m). Since m is boundary and absorbable, we can modify: If m not in A, then after optimal continuation, m is still in B(T_final) (unless T_final includes all cells? If m not absorbed, it remains boundary because it was boundary at T and no boundary lost). Its strength S_m * X < C_start < C_final, so it is absorbable at end. Absorb it, increasing total, contradicting optimality unless m was already absorbed. Thus optimal absorbs m at some point. If m is absorbed at some point, move it to the first action: Need show any sequence that absorbs m later remains feasible if m is moved to front. Let original sequence from T be a1,...,ak,m,... . Consider modified sequence m,a1,...,ak,... . At step for ai in modified, territory contains T ∪ {m} ∪ {a1..a_{i-1}}. Original territory at step for ai contained T ∪ {a1..a_{i-1}}. Since ai was adjacent to original territory, it remains adjacent to superset. Its strength condition: current in modified is C_start + S_m + sum previous a's, which is >= original current C_start + sum previous a's (because S_m >0), so if S_ai * X < original current, then < modified current. Thus feasible. So m first is safe. Thus greedy choosing min absorbable is optimal.

This proof relies on S_m >0. Yes.

What if m is not in optimal set because optimal stops before absorbing all possible? But maximum would include all possible, so if m absorbable at start and remains boundary, can absorb at end. If absorbing m at end might make some previously absorbed cells no longer? No, absorbing only adds. So contradiction. Good.

Thus greedy is correct.

Let's write clean code:
```
import sys
import heapq

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W, X = map(int, input().split())
    P, Q = map(int, input().split())
    S = [list(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(H)]

    sr = P - 1
    sc = Q - 1
    cur = S[sr][sc]

    added = bytearray(H * W)
    added[sr * W + sc] = 1

    heap = []
    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop
    dirs = ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1))

    for dr, dc in dirs:
        nr = sr + dr
        nc = sc + dc
        if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W:
            nid = nr * W + nc
            if not added[nid]:
                added[nid] = 1
                heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nid))

    while heap and heap[0][0] * X < cur:
        val, nid = heappop(heap)
        cur += val
        r = nid // W
        c = nid % W
        for dr, dc in dirs:
            nr = r + dr
            nc = c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W:
                nid2 = nr * W + nc
                if not added[nid2]:
                    added[nid2] = 1
                    heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nid2))

    print(cur)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
This is final.

Pop (0,1) (absorb). Push its neighbors: (0,0) added, (0,2) push, (1,1) push. Now heap has (1,0), (0,2), (1,1). All are boundary? Territory {(0,0),(0,1)}. Boundary: (1,0) adjacent to (0,0); (0,2) adjacent to (0,1); (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) and (1,0)? (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) yes. So all boundary. Good.

No boundary loss.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "heap" containing a cell that is already absorbed? No, because when a cell is popped, it is removed from heap. It won't be pushed again because added true. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "heap" containing a cell that is not boundary? We argued no. Let's test with a small scenario: Start at (0,0). Push (0,1) and (1,0). Pop (0,1) (absorb). Push its neighbors: (0,0) added, (0,2) push, (1,1) push. Now heap has (1,0), (0,2), (1,1). All are boundary? Territory {(0,0),(0,1)}. Boundary: (1,0) adjacent to (0,0); (0,2) adjacent to (0,1); (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) and (1,0)? (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) yes. So all boundary. Good.
If later pop (1,0), push (1,1)? But (1,1) already added, skip. Territory {(0,0),(0,1),(1,0)}. Boundary: (0,2), (1,1), (2,0), (1,? actually (1,1) adjacent to (1,0) and (0,1), boundary). Good.
No boundary loss.

Our algorithm updates cur before checking next. Good.

No boundary loss.

No boundary loss.

Pop (0,1) (absorb). Push its neighbors: (0,0) added, (0,2) push, (1,1) push. Now heap has (1,0), (0,2), (1,1). All are boundary? Territory {(0,0),(0,1)}. Boundary: (1,0) adjacent to (0,0); (0,2) adjacent to (0,1); (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) and (1,0)? (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) yes. So all boundary. Good.
If later pop (1,0), push (1,1)? But (1,1) already added, skip. Territory {(0,0),(0,1),(1,0)}. Boundary: (0,2), (1,1), (2,0), (1,? actually (1,1) adjacent to (1,0) and (0,1), boundary). Good.
No boundary loss.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum possible strength" if he can absorb a slime whose strength is strictly less than 1/X times his strength, but after absorbing, his strength increases, so the condition for next slime uses new strength. Our algorithm updates cur before checking next. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "the gap left by the disappeared slime is immediately filled by Takahashi" and if he absorbs a slime, the slime's cell becomes part of Takahashi, so the slimes adjacent to it become adjacent. Our algorithm pushes neighbors after cur update. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "slimes that were adjacent to the disappeared one (if any) become newly adjacent to Takahashi" If a slime was adjacent to the disappeared one but also adjacent to Takahashi before, it's not newly but remains. Our algorithm skips if already added. Good.

No boundary loss.

Our algorithm updates cur before checking next. Good.

Pop (0,1) (absorb). Push its neighbors: (0,0) added, (0,2) push, (1,1) push. Now heap has (1,0), (0,2), (1,1). All are boundary? Territory {(0,0),(0,1)}. Boundary: (1,0) adjacent to (0,0); (0,2) adjacent to (0,1); (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) and (1,0)? (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) yes. So all boundary. Good.

No boundary loss.

Pop (0,1) (absorb). Push its neighbors: (0,0) added, (0,2) push, (1,1) push. Now heap has (1,0), (0,2), (1,1). All are boundary? Territory {(0,0),(0,1)}. Boundary: (1,0) adjacent to (0,0); (0,2) adjacent to (0,1); (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) and (1,0)? (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) yes. So all boundary. Good.

No boundary loss.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "added" marking when pushing initial neighbors; if a neighbor is pushed, added true. If later another initial neighbor? no duplicates. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "added" marking when pushing neighbors of popped cell; if a neighbor is already in heap, skip. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "heap" containing a cell that is already absorbed? No, because when a cell is popped, it is removed from heap. It won't be pushed again because added true. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "heap" containing a cell that is not boundary? We argued no. Let's test with a small scenario: Start at (0,0). Push (0,1) and (1,0). Pop (0,1) (absorb). Push its neighbors: (0,0) added, (0,2) push, (1,1) push. Now heap has (1,0), (0,2), (1,1). All are boundary? Territory {(0,0),(0,1)}. Boundary: (1,0) adjacent to (0,0); (0,2) adjacent to (0,1); (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) and (1,0)? (1,1) adjacent to (0,1) yes. So all boundary. Good.
If later pop (1,0), push (1,1)? But (1,1) already added, skip. Territory {(0,0),(0,1),(1,0)}. Boundary: (0,2), (1,1), (2,0), (1,? actually (1,1) adjacent to (1,0) and (0,1), boundary). Good.
No boundary loss.

Our algorithm updates cur before checking next. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import sys
import heapq

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline

    H, W, X = map(int, input().split())
    P, Q = map(int, input().split())

    S = [list(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(H)]

    sr = P - 1
    sc = Q - 1
    cur = S[sr][sc]

    added = bytearray(H * W)
    added[sr * W + sc] = 1

    heap = []
    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop
    dirs = ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1))

    for dr, dc in dirs:
        nr = sr + dr
        nc = sc + dc
        if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W:
            nid = nr * W + nc
            if not added[nid]:
                added[nid] = 1
                heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nid))

    while heap and heap[0][0] * X < cur:
        val, nid = heappop(heap)
        cur += val

        r = nid // W
        c = nid % W

        for dr, dc in dirs:
            nr = r + dr
            nc = c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < H and 0 <= nc < W:
                nid2 = nr * W + nc
                if not added[nid2]:
                    added[nid2] = 1
                    heappush(heap, (S[nr][nc], nid2))

    print(cur)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
